--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the foraging mission carefully before designing FSMs.

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for items from ONE half (good half), increases by 1 for items from the OTHER half (bad half)
- Robots need to: go to the good source area, pick up items, return to nest
- WHITE floor = nest, BLACK floor = source area, GRAY = in between
- RED wall = nest side, BLUE/GREEN walls = source sides
- We need robots to prefer one color source over the other (say BLUE over GREEN, or vice versa)

**Key insight:** We don't know which color (BLUE=color 3 or GREEN=color 2) is good. We need to try both. Also need to avoid the bad half.

**Behavior strategy:**
- State for going to source (using GoToColor toward BLUE or GREEN wall)
- State for returning to nest (GoToColor toward RED wall, color=1)
- Transitions based on floor color (BLACK=at source, WHITE=at nest)
- Optionally avoid the bad color source

**Color mapping:** clr: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

Let me design 14 machines with varied strategies:

- 2-state: explore→go to nest, with floor-based transitions
- 3-state: go to blue source → return to nest → explore
- 3-state: go to green source → return to nest → explore
- Variants with different probabilities and transition counts

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to guide my decisions. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and the mission structure, then diversify my approach.

**Reflection on Round 2:**
Without explicit scores, I'll assume the basic 2-state and 3-state GoToColor machines are reasonable baselines. Key uncertainties:
1. Which wall color (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) corresponds to the good half?
2. Do robots need to avoid the bad half actively?

**Refined Strategy:**
- Keep both BLUE and GREEN variants since we don't know which is good
- The best foraging loop: GoToColor(source color) → on BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED/nest) → on WHITE floor → repeat
- Add GoAwayColor from the bad source to avoid score penalties
- Try RED wall (clr=1) for nest navigation since RED wall is on nest side
- Also try pure exploration-based strategies as baselines
- Increase transition probability reliability (higher p values for floor conditions)
- Add 4-state machines with explicit "avoid bad source" state

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received explicit scores from the rounds. I need to reason more carefully about what could be working and what might not be.

**Deep Mission Analysis:**
- Score MINIMISED: delivering "good" items decreases score by 1 (good), delivering "bad" items increases by 1 (bad)
- We need to consistently go to the SAME half of source, not alternate
- The key challenge: robots might wander into the wrong half
- GoToColor toward BLUE (clr=3) or GREEN (clr=2) wall should direct to specific half
- Returning: GoToColor toward RED (clr=1) wall = nest side

**What might be failing:**
- Robots may be spending too much time in exploration states
- The "escape" transition (probability 0.02) from source state might be too rare, meaning robots get stuck
- Need stronger commitment: once on BLACK floor, immediately switch to return mode
- The nest detection (WHITE floor) needs to be very reliable

**New ideas:**
- Try GoAwayColor from the bad wall while heading to source - creates directional preference
- Simpler 2-state machines might be more robust
- Try higher rwm values for more directed exploration
- Try attraction to other robots as a way to aggregate at source

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'm not receiving explicit scores, which is unusual. Let me reason more carefully about what the best strategy should be and diversify significantly.

**Critical Rethinking:**

The mission says "an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1." We need to figure out which is which AND consistently deliver from the good half.

**Key insight I may have missed:** The robots can perceive colored WALLS with their camera. BLUE walls are behind one half, GREEN behind the other. If we go toward BLUE (clr=3) and it's the good half, score decreases. If GREEN (clr=2) is good, we should go there instead.

**What I haven't tried enough:**
1. Pure GoAwayColor from one wall color (pushes robot to opposite half)
2. Using LED colors to signal item-carrying state for coordination
3. Very simple 1-state machines as baseline
4. Exploration-only as sanity check
5. Combinations where robots in nest state avoid going back to wrong source

**New topology ideas:**
- State 0: GoToColor(BLUE) → BLACK floor → State 1 (return)
- State 1: GoToColor(RED) → WHITE floor → State 0
- Also try GoAwayColor(GREEN) as alternative to GoToColor(BLUE)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'm still not getting explicit scores back, which means I need to think more carefully about what's likely working and what isn't.

**Deep Rethink:**

The core foraging loop is: GoToSource → pick up item (automatic on entering BLACK floor) → GoToNest → deliver (automatic on entering WHITE floor) → repeat.

The critical question: which color wall (BLUE=clr3 or GREEN=clr2) is the "good" source? Since we don't know, we must try both and let evolution select.

**What I may be getting wrong:**
- GoToColor toward RED wall (clr=1) for nest navigation - but RED is the wall COLOR, not the floor. The camera sees wall blocks. This should work.
- However, maybe the RED wall isn't visible from the source area due to arena geometry
- Alternative: GoAwayColor from source wall could push robots toward nest
- The transition from source state to return state triggers on BLACK floor, but robots START on BLACK - they need to have already entered and gotten an item. The BLACK floor detection should still work since they need to travel there first.

**New approach: Try GoAwayColor from source walls to return home**

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Serious Rethink Required**

After 6 rounds without feedback scores, I need to radically reconsider. The lack of score data suggests perhaps my FSMs are all performing similarly (possibly around 0 or poorly). Let me think from scratch about what could go wrong.

**Fundamental Issue:** I've been assuming GoToColor toward RED wall (clr=1) works for nest navigation. But wait - the LED colors in the format use {0,4,5,6} but clr for GoToColor uses {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Let me verify: clr=1 is red, clr=2 is green, clr=3 is blue. That seems right.

**Another critical issue:** When robots start in GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN), they're starting from the middle. They might overshoot into the wrong half. Also, the "item replacement" mechanic means entering wrong half replaces good item with bad one.

**New strategy - use GoAwayColor from RED wall to reach source:**
- GoAwayColor(RED, clr=1) → moves AWAY from nest → toward source
- Once on BLACK, switch to GoToColor(RED) to return

**Also trying:** What if the scoring is set up so BLUE is bad and GREEN is good, or vice versa? I need to commit to both and also try neutral exploration strategies.

**Key new idea:** A robot doing pure exploration might score near 0 (random between halves). To score negative we need systematic preference for one color. Let me try more aggressive avoidance of the wrong color.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Critical Rethink After 7 Rounds**

I've been running many rounds without seeing scores. I need to think about what's fundamentally different about this problem.

**Key realization I've been missing:**
The scoring says "an item from one half decreases score by 1, an item from the other half increases by 1." We don't know WHICH half is good. But the arena description says BLUE walls are behind one half, GREEN behind the other. The problem is SYMMETRIC - we can't know a priori which is good.

**What this means:** ANY consistent strategy that always picks from ONE half will score either very negative (great) or very positive (bad). A random strategy scores near 0. The optimizer will find which color is good from the simulation results.

**What might actually be failing:**
- My GoToColor(RED) for nest return - is RED (clr=1) actually perceivable? The wall is red but the camera perceives colored objects. This should work.
- Maybe the issue is robots get "stuck" at the source because BLACK floor detection fires immediately when they arrive, before they move deep enough to reliably get an item
- Maybe I need to use a DELAY (probability-based transition) after arriving at source before returning

**New approach: Add a dwell time at source using low-probability transitions, and try completely different topologies including pure attraction/repulsion.**

Also trying: GoToColor toward BLUE while GoAwayColor from GREEN simultaneously - using 2 states only with very clean transitions.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Deep Analysis After 8 Rounds Without Score Feedback**

I'm clearly not getting explicit scores, which means I need to think about this more carefully. Let me reconsider the entire problem structure.

**What I know for certain:**
1. Score is minimized - negative scores are best
2. Good items decrease score by 1, bad items increase by 1
3. BLUE (clr=3) walls behind one half, GREEN (clr=2) behind other
4. WHITE floor = nest, BLACK floor = source, GRAY = transit
5. RED wall (clr=1) on nest side

**Fundamental problem with my designs:**
- I keep using GoToColor(RED, clr=1) for nest return. But clr=1 is RED. Is the wall color index correct? Let me recheck: clr options are {1,2,3,4,5,6}. The color mapping isn't explicitly stated in the spec beyond saying "target colour." I've been ASSUMING clr=1=red, clr=2=green, clr=3=blue. This assumption may be WRONG.

**Alternative color mappings to try:**
- Maybe clr=4=yellow, clr=5=magenta, clr=6=cyan for the walls
- Or maybe the wall colors don't match the camera color indices at all

**Better approach:** Use FLOOR COLOR only for navigation, not wall colors:
- On GRAY floor: explore/move toward source
- On BLACK floor: immediately return (GoToColor toward something, or just explore back)
- On WHITE floor: switch to source-seeking mode

**Also:** Try using neighbor-based transitions and attraction behaviors as completely different approach.

**Most robust simple strategy:** 
- State 0: Explore with high rwm (wander toward source)
- Transition on BLACK → State 1 (return to nest via GoToColor RED wall or just explore)  
- Transition on WHITE → State 0

Let me also try: what if robots just need to avoid GREEN walls entirely?

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Final Round - Last Chance Analysis**

After 9 rounds without explicit scores, I need to make my best educated guess about what works. Let me think about what the optimizer is actually doing - it's running each FSM multiple times and averaging scores. Without feedback, I'll maximize diversity while keeping the best candidates from my design space.

**What I believe should work best:**
The core loop is: GoToColor(good_source) → BLACK floor detected → GoToColor(RED_nest) → WHITE floor detected → repeat.

The two key unknowns are: (1) which color is good (BLUE=clr3 or GREEN=clr2), and (2) whether GoToColor(RED=clr1) actually navigates to the nest.

**Final strategy:**
- Keep the best-looking designs from previous rounds (both BLUE and GREEN variants)
- Add completely new topologies I haven't tried: using floor-color transitions WITHOUT GoToColor at all (pure exploration with floor-triggered returns)
- Try GoAwayColor from source walls as the return mechanism
- Try very simple 1-state exploration as sanity check
- Maximize transitions richness with 3-4 per state

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
